In the course of a conversation with Mr. Willoughby (CP)
Mr. Locke of Mr. Donald Nelson's office mentioned that Mr. O.
R. Johnson of wPB was making a study on Japan for Mr. Nelson.
Mr. Willoughby told him that he was chairman of an intra-
departmental committee on Japanese economic affairs, and
that he would like to exchange views with Mr. Johnson in
order to avoid duplication of work. Mr. Johnson later tele-
phoned that he was holding a meeting of some officials work-
ing on Japanese problems, and it was arranged for me to at-
C
tend.
S/H
Mr. Johnson stated that at the request of Mr. Donald
工
Nelson he was undertaking a study of the historical de-
U.
velopment of industry in modern Japan, especially the
growth of the various industries. This was to be used
in connection with some other studies, already made, of
the United States and Russia, in an effort to estimate
what might be reasonable to expect in any given period
of time in China. Mr. Johnson said it was necessary to
complete his work within a very short period and he was
anxious to find any studies which had been, or were being,
made along these lines.
There
1945
R

=== Page 4 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
There was some canvassing of the situation by the
persons present regarding studies already available. It
appeared that most of the recent work in Government of-
fices had been pointed towards current conditions rather
than towards historical growth.
Published books, none of them quite meeting the re-
quirement, were suggested, and one person from eaoh ageney
undertook to canvass his own agency for further possibili-
ties. (The undersigned is anxious to receive information
about available material.)
Mr. Walker reported that the Department of Commerce
had a collection of published studies of Japan's national
income.
Mr. Martin stated that Oss was working on estimates
of national income, but that they would not be ready for
some months.
Mr. Anderberg spoke of a report on Japan's ship build-
ing and shipping industries which was in process in the
Tariff Commission.
While not very familiar with completed work in the
Department, I suggested that some of FMA's studies would
have important bearing on Mr. Johnson's problem. The
various reports of commercial attachés were referred to,
and I also mentioned that other studies being made in
various Divisions of ECA might have important historical
bearings.
There was a brief disoussion of some of the differ-
ences between China and Japan which would bear upon the
relative speed of industrialization in the two countries.
I did not bring the matter up at the time but subse-
quently suggested that Mr. Johnson might find it useful
to contact the Federal Reserve Board in regard to work
that it is doing on Japan.
CP: DHB: LD

Document on Economic Policy Toward Japan
Mr. Collado's memorandun to you under date of
August 7, 1944 engaged to bring a paper on long-run
economic objectives with respect to Japan to the atten-
tion of the Inter-Divisional Area Committee on the Far
East. I am now sending you a copy of a document entitled,
"Economic Policy Toward Japan: Summary Statement", which
has been prepared in the Division of Financial and Monetary
894.50/1-1045
Affairs, and which has been cleared in the Office of
Economic Affairs.
You are no doubt aware of the various circumstances
which have delayed the preparation of this paper. Even
now its recommendations must be regarded as tentative and
not final, subject to modification as may be required to
bring them into line with the results of detailed support-
ing studies now being undertaken and with policy as
determined at high government levels.
I should appreciate receiving the comments of the
Area Committee on the substance of this paper at as early
a date as practicable, in order that its submission to the
Executive Committee on Economic Foreign Policy may be
expedited.

TANDARD FORM NO. 64
FmA- wh.Crelado
TO
: ECA - Mr. Hdley
OC/R
entral
DIVISION OF F
SUBJECT: Document on Economic Policy toward Japan
JAN 2 4 1945
This memorandum strikes me as unsatisfactory in several
ways, but I have no objection at this stage in the considera-
tion of the problem to its circulation for discussion.
The main source of my dissatisfaction is the apparent
absence of any firm notion of the kind of Japan - political,
economic and social - which we would like to see emerge
from her impending defeat. This failure accounts for such minor
inconsistencies as the recommendation that the economic power
of the great family corporations be reduced (pp. 6-7) and the
894.50/1-2245
recommendation (p. 8) that the Japanese government be required
to compensate its nationals for foreign property seized on
reparations account; and such major omissions as the failure
to consider the long-range political and economic implications
of the recommendation that the occupation authorities accept
responsibility for preventing the development of "drastically
unmanageable economic situation" and for "the restoration of
essential facilities of trade and transportation."
Attachments
1.
1-12.
CS
S/HM

This memorandum is conoerned with the urgent need for
olarification of the respective fields of responsibility of
various groups within the Department that are concerned with
economic problems in the Pacific area. These groups include
89
the Far Eastern Area Committee, the working group on Japanese
4.50
reparations, and the ECA Committee on Japan. A brief review
of the present status of studies in this field will serve to
demonstrate the need for clarification.
1. Two papers passed by the Area Committee dealt, respeot-
ively, with short-run and longer-run economio policies toward
Japan. Both had been considered by our "economic suboommittee"
1
which included representatives not only of ECA, but also of
2
WEA, FE, and TS. The Area Committee not unnaturally took the
3
view that decisions of its subcommittee were subject to its
4
revision in detail. ECA was not satisfied with certain features5
of these papers as passed and proposed amendments to the one
on longer-run pcl1cy (CAC-l60) that were 1ntended to bring it
more nearly into line with an ECEFP paper on economic polioy
toward Germany (D-36-44). These amendments were apparently
not pleasing to some members of the Area Committee, whioh has
taken no action on them.
Meanwhile, the ECEFP requested that a paper similar in
scope and content to D-36/44 be prepared with reference to
Japan for submission to that Committee at an early date.
Mr. Collado advised Mr. Blakeslse of that request and engaged
C
to bring the draft of the new and longer paper to the attention
of the Area Committee. Completion of the paper was delayed for
want of definite indication of polioy at high levels, and the
Area Committee has shown some impatience over the delay. It
1s understood that the completed paper has now been referred
to you by Mr. Haley, together with a draft memorandum of trans-
mittal to Mr. Blakeslee.
8
Although the paper is admittedly provisional, it 1s never
theless believed that its suggestions are consonant with United
h
States economio policy so far as now apparent, and that the
U1
paper therefore affords a guide for the consideration of more
C
specifio

-2 -
speoifio problems associated with its general theme, study
of whioh is now contemplated under the ECA Committee on Japan.
It is further believed that it should be forwarded to the Far
Eastern Area Committee for comment on substanoe in order that
it may be brought into harmony, if that appears necessary,
with general politioal policy toward Japan.
Subsequently, the Committee on Postwar Programs requested
revision of the paper on short-run polioy (CAC-222). Inasmuoh
as the paper was not striotly confined to the short-run, ECA
proposed that further aotion be postponed pending considera-
tion of the new paper on longer-run polioy, and the drafting
officer now appears to have conourred in that proposal. There
is thus additional reason for now transmitting the new paper
to the Area Committee. Furthermore, its transmittal to the
Committee for comment on substanoe will help in itself to olar-
1fy the respective fields of responsibility of the Area Committee
and ECA.
2. An informal working group on Japanese reparations was
constituted some time ago under FMA and inoluded among its
members individuals from other agencies as well as from the
Department. Various phases of the problem were assigned to
membere who were believed best equipped to deal with them, and
some papers have been completed. The responsibility for con-
tinuing this work also needs to be defined with reference not
only to the area Committee but also to the newly oreated ECA
Committee on Japan.
3. In commenting on a proposal, originating in FMA, to

Mr. Luthringer proposed on November 3, 1944, a steering committee

was referred to Mr. Haley, to whom also Messrs. Gay and Willoughby
later submitted proposals for an ECA committee on Japan, togother
with agenda. Suoh a oommittee has now been authorized and its
chairmanship has fallen to Mr. Willoughby. Its funotion is
the general direotion and supervision of economio studies on
Japan, and papers prepared under its supervision are likewise
to be laid before the Far Eastern Area Committee for comment
on substance.

Dear Mr. Grew:-
RECORDO
894.50/1-2345
Possibly you remember me as one of two missionaries from Korea who
called upon you in Tokyo in 1939 to discuss with you what was happening
to missionary interests in Korea during the period when Japan was beginn-
ing to "squeeze out" all American interests in her Empire.
In company with a number of others I was compelled at last to sever
my connections with the Boerd I formerly served because its attitude was
one of acquiesence in idolatry and also one which would lead Japan to go
further in its overbearing attitude to all American interests
This illustrates the fact that there was even before Pearl Harbor a
sharp division of conviction among missionaries in the Empire as to the
demands Japan was making upon all Americans, but especially upon Christians,
including missionaries.
That division of conviction still persists. On January 6, 1945, a group
of missionaries made a public statement in New York pleading for lenient
treetment of Japan. That statement seems to many others to be very unfortu-
nate, and for that reason another group has prepared the statement of which
I enclose a copy.
S
Many who have signed the statement I enclose have spent many years in
the Japanese Empire, and many of them actively opposed the pacifism and
lack of conviction so sadly prevalent among many other missionaries. That
pacifism, in our opinion, was actually a contributing factor to the war
that eventually came, in that it helped to lead Japan to believe that she
could do what she would and that Americans and the American government would
go no further than verbal protest in any case.
n AE 
We who have signed this statement do not believe there can be freedom
from menace for any neighboring nation, and certainly not the real religious
liberty in which we are especially interested, as long as the present govern-
ment of Japan, centered in State Shinto and militarism, continues to exist.
50
We therefore oppose the statement issued in New York, some of whose
sighers are known to have condoned the Japanese government's demands upon
1.2345
the whole Christian Church of the Empire and of subjected lands, by which
measures, and those which Japan has adopted subsenuently, the Christian
Chirch as such in Japan has been forced to become a part of one patriotic
religious body composed of Shintoists, Buddhists, and other non-Christian
religions. This new religious body evidently includes the one union

=== Page 13 === (confidence 0.98)
Honorable Joseph C. Grew - January 23, 1945.
Page 2.
Protestant Church of Japan and the Roman Catholics, all pledged to utmost
effort to "destroy the blasphemous religious ideals of America and Britain,"
as a Tokyo broadcast picked up by the F.C.C. puts it.
Those whom I represent do not believe that such a government, dominated
by militarism and by State Shinto, which is practically the deification of
Japan, should be allowed to continue to exist. We are loyal supporters of
the United States of America and her Allies to the point of absolute surrender
of Japan and the reconstruction of her government in a way we believe will be
found to be necessary to the peace of the world and even to the freedom of the
Japanese people themselves.
We are not giving this statement to the press because we do not want to
create eabarrassment to our own government or possible difficulties for
Americans who may now be in the control of Japan. I am, however, sending a
copy of our statement, together with a letter similar to this to the
President and to the Secretaries of State, of War and of the Navy because
we believe this is a most serious matter involving many American interests in
the Orient in the future, including any real liberty even to Japanese subjects.
If you should desire further information as to my relationship to this
whole matter Mr. Wn. R. Langdon, former Consul-General in Seoul. (Keijo) Korea.
and now in the State Department, would be very glad to give you such information.
Sincerely yours,
JoordouHolderf
J. Gordon Holdcroft

=== Page 14 === (confidence 0.98)
MISSIONARY STATEMENT RE
PHACE PROPOSALS FOR JAPAN.
According to news despatches, a group of twenty-three missionaries from Japan.
Korea, China and the Philippines have issued a pronouncement againet the proposals
widely made for a "hard" peace for Japan. Their statement says that these proposals
"seem to call for ruthless destruction of life and property until unconditiomal
surrender is followed by a military occupation." They advocate an international
authority whose jurisdiction would extend to all nations, victors and vanquiabed
alike, and which would make unnecessary "this drastic surgery of Japanese mation-
hood." This statement was made public by Dr. Charles W. Iglehart of Union Theo-
logical Seminary. New York City.
Such a statement does not represent the point of view of Bible-believing mission-
aries who believe that acceptance of Jesus Christ as Saviour and Lord demands unre-
lenting opposition to worship by Christians at the shrines of the alleged sun-goddess
of Japan. The Japanese military dominated government is identified with Japanese
National Shinto. N tional Shinto teaches that the Japanese emperor is divine, that
it is the destiny of Japan to rule the rest of the world, and that that rule is to
be accomplished by military measures. Can we expect a just and lasting peace with
a government that has such ideas and is in a position to enforce them with military
powert
In seeking to accomplish world domination, Japan has attacked America. If
that National Shinto military government is left in control of Japan after the War,
we can expect another war of agression to follow in twenty-five or fifty years.
as soon as Japan recovers from the present conflict.
We, therefore, missionaries who have lived in the Orient, or mission executives
having interests there, affirm our belief that God is the final arbiter of conflicts.
but we believe that, humanly speaking, we can expect no lasting peace as long as the
present National Shinto military government rules Japan, for that government not
only denies all religious liberty to its subject peoples, but menaces the peace of
the world.
SIGNED:BY:
Nission Board Officers and Executives:-

Area Committee attitude Toward Paper on

Mr. Ballantine desired to discuss certain aspects of
this question with me. He spoke well of the paper but
suggested that it could scarcely serve as a reply to the
Centrel
0
many specific questions asked by the Army and Navy. After
some discussion he said that he would prefer to regard this
paper as setting forth basic policy for guidance of the
Department in answering such questions, and that the answers
should be prepared either in FE or in FMA, according to
6
circumstances. I said that I would cooperate with such a
plan.
Mr. Ballantine further said that he wished this paper
0
to be discussed in the Area Committee only from the point of
view of its substance and not from that of form, to which
2
Retummed
of course I agreed.
3
Mr. Ballantine was very anxious to make it clear that FE
and the Area Committee wished to defer to the economic officers on
questions of economic policy, although he considered that FE
was responsible for seeing that that policy was in harmony
with general political policy. Finally, he apologized for
the delay in considering the paper in committee, which I have
understood from Mr. Blakeslee is the result of specially
CS/A
urgent work growing out of the interest of the House Committee
on Naval Affairs on the Mandated Islands.
It had seemed desirable to me that you should be present
when the Area Committee discusses this paper; not only have I
on several occasions told the Committee that you were responsi-
ble for the paper in accordance with your memorandum of last
August to Mr. Blakeslee, but I had felt that certain members
of the Committee had already taken an unfavorable attitude
on the question of jurisdiction. In the light of Mr. Ballantine's
remarks, however, the question of jurisdiction should not
trouble greatly.' I shall advise you when the Committee is
894.50
prepared to discuss the question and you may then determine
whether you will attend.

FROM
O. C. Lockhart
SUBJECT:
Paper on "Ecpnomic Policy Toward Japan"
Mr. Dooman called on me yesterday to discuss this paper,
with which he expressed general, though not complete, assent.
He was particularly concerned with its failure to provide
specific instructions for application to particular situations
I told him I thought this resulted from the character of the
paper which was based generally on the German document, as
requested by ECEFP. He insisted that specification would be
necessary though he thought it might be in other or supplementaf
papers, and suggested that this should not be regarded by the
economic officers as the definitive paper on Japan. I told
him that I had had no such thought and cited the letter of
transmittal to Mr. Blakeslee in support. He then suggested
that the Area Committee might proceed to have other papers
894.50/
prepared which would answer specific and detailed questions
which he assumed would be presented by Army and Navy. I
replied that I did not feel in position to say whether the
Area Committee or the economic officers should prepare these
more detailed papers, although I realized that the contact
with the Army and Navy was more direct through FE. In this
connection he referred to the State, Army and Navy Coordinating
2
Committee and to the problems before them.
1
Finally, I suggested that the Area Committee might, if
645
it saw fit, either make a minute or pass a resolution to be
presented to Mr. Clayton or Mr. Mason approving the paper as
setting forth basic policy for guidance of the Department in
answering specific questions. Mr. Dooman approved this
suggestion and indicated that he would make such a proposal
to the Committee.
I found it hard to avoid the feeling that there was some
jurisdictional interest in Mr. Dooman's comments, particularly
since he referred to specific papers as if they were to be
prepared in the Area Committee and in part because Mr.
Ballantine had on February 3 suggested that specific papers
C
should be prepared either in FE or in FMA.

Dear Mr. Grew:
Depariment of Stata
We thought you might be interested in reading a copy of
894.50/3-145
our letter of August 9, 194l addressed to the then
Ambassador of Japan, Admiral Kichisaburo Nomura. This
copy is herewith enclosed.
We predicted at that time, before the Pearl Harbor
"Incident", that we would be at war with Japan and when
that time arrived we would destroy their cities by fire.
That prediction is now rapidly taking place.
In that same letter to Admiral Nomura we advised him
that when our war with Japan was over and their cities
had been destroyed that we would like the job of re-
building those cities for Japan.
We realize that in all bombed-out areas, whether it be
in Japan, China or Europe that the people in these
territories, having lost everything through the war,
will not be able to finance themselves to a new start.
We desire to ask you whether or not the United States
Government has any plan, or expects to have a plan, for
financing the rebuilding of some of these areas that
tave been destroyed by bombing and fires, and if such a
plan is contemplated that you kindly advise us concerning
F
the same. If no such plan is contemplated, we ask your
advice and suggestion as to where and how The Aladdin
Company could finance the huge amount of money it would
  n
take for such purposes.
We have the plans, the specifications, and the experience
to organize and put over huge building operations based
on our Readi-Cut System of manufacturing houses by machin-
ery so that they can be erected by all classes of people
in all parts of the world.
AIADDIN
HOMEBUILDERS TO THE NATION OVER THIRIY THREE YEARS

=== Page 29 === (confidence 0.99)
Mr. Joseph C. Grew -- 2
3/1/45
This Government will certainly be hard pressed to put to
work the millions of unemployed that we are bound to have.
The greatest opportunity to put great quantities of sol-
diers to work, as well as private individuals, is in the
erection of their own homes which have been manufactured
by machinery and which they can themselves erect.
An investment of this kind 1s the best kind of security
there is for the advancement of Government funds, and we
would like to seriously cooperate with the State Depart-
ment and our Washington Government in handling big house
construction work after the war is over.
Any information or suggestions you can give us on this
subject will be very much appreciated.
Yours very truly,
THE ALADDIN COMPANY

Dear Admiral Nomura:
In these hectic times Japanese cities may soon be wiped
out by fire. After the war is over, the Japanese Govern-
ment will probably decide to rebuild their cities by
0
modern methods. The most logical place for the houses to
come from for this purpose would be from the United States
and the most logical firm to take charge of the rehousing
of the Japanese people under Japanese Government supervision,
should be our Company.
The money for this purpose might be arranged for with our
P
Washington Government. While our Government is now busy
financing many foreign governments, we feel confident that
after the war is overwith, our Washington Government would
give serious consideration to financing the rebuilding of
the cities of Japan.
Kindly permit us to bring to your attention at this time
The Aladdin Company and our Aladdin Houses.
By way of introduction we wish to explain that The Aladdin
Company originated the Readi-Cut System of manufacturing
and shipping our Aladdin Readi-Cut Houses.
Starting about twenty years ago and continuing on thereafter,
The Aladdin Company was favored with orders for their
Aladdin houses from some of Japans largest and most in-
fluential corporations. In fact Admiral Togo lived and
died in an Aladdin house shipped to and erected in Tokyo.
Some of the corporations we have received orders from in
past years include such well known Japanese firms as:
Mitsui & Co.
Yukio Arishima
Takata & Co.
J. Taumara
Yuji Sawada & Co.
Macondray & Co.
Mitsubishi Saeji Kaisha Ltd.
T. Shikata & Co.
and others.

Dear Mr. Grew:
ansind
my
Now that the larger cities of Japan are being razed, the capitula-
tion of Japan proper may not be far distant, and it is time to write this
letter. You will not remember me, but I saw you often and shook hands
with you once in Tokio during the Summer of 1936. (Do you remember how
Admiral Yarnell lectured the Japanese Navy at the banquet given to our
visiting fleet?) But I kept away from the Embassy, because if the purely
private researches I pursued had been known to the Japanese government,
it was best that you should know nothing of them.
Between March and September of 1936 I tried to discover whether
and how Japan might be diverted from its headlong course toward war. As
you know, the die was already cast when the February incident allowed
Terauchi and his associates to seize all the portfolios and strings of
CS/EG
government. But I continued my inquiries, covering all levels of the
Japanese hierarchy from ordinary citizens through educational, military,
business, and diplomatic circles, until finally I reached M. Matsuda,
former ambassador to Rome and Brussels, and et that time advisor on foreign
relations to the Emperor, whom he met thrice weekly. With M. Matsuda I had
more than one long and disturbing conversation. I met him through his son-
in-law Yoshi Kagami of the Mitsubishi clan, whose wife was related to the
families of the assassinated ministers. In this way I learned much of what
lay behind the political scene.
I made no report to Washington on my return to the United States,
being assured that our Government was perfectly well informed of what went
on in Japan through you. Nor have I published any account of my findings
and conclusions regarding Japan, since these might have endangered or
embarrassed my informents there.
Frankly, my desire is to be of service in the determination of the
directives governing the future of Japan. I have no useful knowledge of
the Japanese language, but since I was a boy I have given considerable study
to its history and culture. I am persuaded that Japan, in spite of its
present limitations, can and should play a role in the future world, and that
its enactment of this role is wholly dependent upon the friendship and guidance
894.50/3-1145
of the United States. I believe that Japan will recover from this war mor
easily and quickly than China will reach political stability. Given our  lend
E
nat.
R2
Cat.
Dist.

=== Page 33 === (confidence 0.99)
2. Grew - Miller
March 12, 1945
rather a support to us in the fulfillment of our Far East policies. There
are good men in Japan; I know some of them and can find more. If these men
can be gathered together and wisely instructed, we shall have done much for
the future development of Japan and for world peace.
It seems to me that whatever service I may be able to render would
be best utilized in an advisory and perhaps anonymous capacity rather than
in any official or administrative position. What I would like to do is to
bring together the group of political, educational end business leaders
whose liberal government would advance both the interests of peace and the
interests of the Japanese people. I am perturbed by some recent statements
in the public press as to what should be our future policies toward Japan.
I would say that the prestige of the Emperor can be made a temporary asset
in the direction of Japan toward popular government, and that those who
advocete violent end radical revolution at this time show little understand-
ing of Japan and little real concern for our relations with that people and
with Russia.
Short of such effective service as I have indicated, I would prefer
to remain here, where I have work that I love in philosophy and in the
rehabilitation of religion in our intellectual and academic life. My
governmentel service would presumably be temporary.
Believing that the future peace of the world depends on a solid
relationship between the U.S.S.R. and ourselves, and that this relation-
ship is affected more directly by what happens in the Far East than by
Europeen eventualities, importent though these are, I am glad to know that
the immediate responsibility for our Pacific policies is in your hands.
Yours very sincerely,
Nhigh Miller.
Hugh Miller
(Professor of Philosophy, The University
of California at Los Angeles. Author
of History end Science, Christian Truth
in History, etc.)

DATE: March 12,
8
FROM :
O. C. Lockhart
SUBJECT:
Economic Policy Toward Japan
My notes indicate that at a meeting of the Area Committee
on February 17 ir. Blakeslee indicated with reference to
section VI on page 1l a desire to limit controls to a period
as long as necessary to establish democratic government. He
stated that in early recommendations of the Committee this
thought had been indicated by referring to the future Japan
as "member in the family of nations", but said that this phrase
had been deleted on Mr. Long's objection. Mr. Blakeslee thought
the paragraph assumed permanent subjection of Japan and suggested
that he wouid be satisfied with some such phrase as, "control
will run for some period of years beyond military occupation".
There was general dissent to this suggestion and, after con-
8
siderable discussion, the Committee appeared to approve
6
phraseology suggested by r. Ballantine reading as follows:
94.50
"Notwithstanding the fact that the ultimate aim of
the United States is a Japan properly discharging
50/3
its responsibilities in the family of nations,
there must be envisaged the fact that the control
of the Japanese capacity to wage war, which will
3-1245
begin with disarmament, must necessarily extend
beyond the period of military occupation."
I may add that the official minutes are less specific with
regard to Mr. Blakeslee's comment than my own notes.
I contended that the statement in my text with regard to
C
the continuance of control beyond the period of occupation was
S
obviously true,and referred to the section on time periods.
H
Perhaps partly as a result of that reference, but also because
of Mr. Dooman's suggestion that the word "current" appearing
M
in the first sentence of the second paragraph should be changed
to "continuing" (this change suggested to me the very point
to which Mr. Blakeslee objected), query was raised as to the
relation of these two paragraphs to the time periods described
in Section III.
I stated that the first paragraph was
primarily concerned with the first period of military occupation,
and the second paragraph concerned primarily with the third period,
but
G
0
1
3
2
5

=== Page 37 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
but that it had proved impossible to make a rigid distinction;
the several periods inevitably overlapped more or less. At
the meeting on Saturday (March 10) Mr. Borton sent in a re-
draft of the section which did not seem to me to be satisfactory.
There was, however, considerable discussion around the table,
largely by members new to the meetings, and the Chairman
repeatedly suggested that political considerations were
involved. So many and such conflicting suggestions for amend-
ment were suggested that it appeared to me hopeless to do
anything about it at that time. Mr. Ballantine was appealed
to as a master-rephraser but he, likewise, confessed himself
confused by the various suggestions. At the close of the meet-
ing the matter was left open by the Chairman's statement that
the Committee hoped I wouid find a way of emphasizing the
successive time periods in this section of the paper more
clearly than had yet been done, and that I might confer with
Messrs. Ballantine and Dooman on this point.
At the meeting on Friday, in response to the Chairman's
inquiry as to my views of the Committee's suggestions, I said
that I thought there was very little ground for the Committee's
contention concerning time periods. It was suggested that
the sentence having to do with electric power plants was better
placed in the first paragraph than in the second, and at the
time that seemed to me to be possibly true; although it had
been removed from the first paragraph as a result of earlier
suggestions in the Committee. It now developed that some of
the new members were unable to distinguish between the reference
in the first sentence to the control of current operations and
that to the control of electric power. On reconsideration in
a quieter environment, however, it appears to me, and I so
stated at the meeting on Saturday, that the sentence on electric
power control might be placed in either paragraph because both
had some reference to the second time period.
There was also some reference at Saturday's meeting to
import control and its dependence on the support of exporting
countries. Mr. Dooman appeared to argue somewhat along the
lines advanced a month earlier by Mr. Blaisdell that control
of this sort (eyen though so obviously of a post-war or post-
peace character) could not be conducted through the international
organization even to the extent of obtaining the cooperation of

=== Page 40 === (confidence 0.95)
I
Another source of reparation may be found in Japanese
public property abroad. The several United Nations should be
free to sequestrate and liquidate property rights and interests
belonging to Japan or its nationals, individual or corporate,
within the territories of the respective United Nations,
including territories ceded to them subject to later decision
as to whether and how compensation should be made to private
Arcident
owners. Small holdings of individual Japanese should not be
disturbed.
The foregoing provisions should apply also to
Japanese property in Korea and in the former Japanese Mandated
Islands. Such property might be placed under the trusteeship
of one or more of the United Nations until the ultimate dis-
position of such property has been determined. Policy in
respect to the sequestration and compensation for such property
should if possible be uniform among the United Nations. The
military government in occupied areas should assume control
of such sequestrated properties; and should retain control of
them in such areas as Formosa and Korea pending decision as to
their disposition.

The Controlling Syndioates
As it has been mentioned before, the chief point of the establish-
mont of Japanese industries is no other than the maintenanse of the
produstive onaraoter of our whole industrial eiroles during the rise of
oapitalism and the realisation of a country which can prevent the menopely
of eapital. It will be satisfaotory if we can help and encourage the

industrial capital rampantly on the other hand, and lead their eapacity
of industrial management into the control of medium and amall mubordinate
industries under the government.
The basis of this thought is not liberalism. neither is it
totalitarianism. It is based on a Japanese idea which may be ealled life
econony with a continuous proeess of the ciroulation of capital and the
uptringing of industries. The trees and grass suok up nurition from the
earth to keep a luxuriant growth of the branches and leaves, and develop
into green forests or & grassy fields. At the same time, they never fail
to drop branehes. leaves. and fruit on the ground every year for the
preservation of the fertility of the earth and the propagation of their
seeds. There is a continuous process of life. It is right for the
tremondous industrial capital to follow the control of the state, return a
share of its profit as fertilizer to the people, whioh is the earth of
the state, through tax payment, and use the rest for expansion or repro-
duotion and the upbringing of the goneral medium and mmali industries
without letting the industrial capisal become monopolistio capital
restraining the finance of the state. Monopolized capital does not wish
to return its branohes, leaves, and fruit to the earth; it deems the bonds
and finanoial profit as its life, so its destiny would be withering and
death after it suoks up all the nutrition; but in attempting to esoape
from death. it is going to suok up even the earth itself. The present
World War is brought about by BRITAIN and AERICA in their notorious
struggle of mueh kind. Communism is equal to a prinoiple which denies
the natural law of the growth of branohes and leaves of trees and grass
and the ripening of fruit, but thinks that trees and grass can be made
artifioially and fruit is available thereby. The truth exists neither
in the parts nor in the whole. It almost always exists in its prosess.
wo want to bring into reality a country in whioh the industries are
always in a prooess of development, with the medium and smell indmstries
duly brought up and the powerful capital restrained, in whion the oapital
is always ciroulating both upward and downward, so that a full dovelopment

When the ramous Gerzan geologist kIChfnureN was engaged in goologioal

coal are extromely alundant, so he expeotation ibr their future devolopment oan

Sinoe then, many soholars and technioal experts of different countries
oame in turn for researoh, and consequently. it was found out that the iron
resouroes which hicillnotn oalled inexaustible were mostly of bad quality. that
they were sporadically distributed, and that there was little possibility for
development. However. the problem of iron in this vast country. whioh has a
population of apout one fifth of the total population of the world, furnishes
various themes of general interest and importance for researoh. together with
the problem of coal.
Now, in order to make a detailed geologioal investigation of the present
condition of the undsrground resouroes on the continent. which is vast and almost
boundless, of the waount of underground reserves and ore beds. we oan only wait
for the establiament of a special organization for investigation. At the beginning
of tne Taisno ore (1912 - 1925) a geologioal investigation station was established
in the Department of Agriculture and Commeroe in China under the above mentioned
condition. e may presume that geologioal researon on minerals in the country
tegan to step into a new era fron that time. The government engaged learned
authorities and teonnical experts from EunrE and EhlCA, and exerted efforts in
the investigation oi minerais anu resouroes. Especially wnen AnDansoN, the
Direotor of the wedisn Geologloal Investigation Station. was employed as an

investigations were started speedily with the approval of the central gevernment.

Classifioation and Distribution of Iron Doposits in CHINA
Iron resources in CHINa oan be olassified soientifioally into soven
types, as follows;
1. Contaot Deposits (接觸鑛床)
2.
Thermo-Aqueous Doposits (?) (熱 水  床)
3.
stratified Doposits(成屠费床)
4.
Residue Deposits (殘留床)
Dynamic Metamorphism Deposits (動力变質鑛床)
5.
6.
Ignoous Doposits (火成鑛床)
7. Alluvial Deposita (漂砂广床)
Among these, minerals belonging to No. 1. (contact daposits) No. 2.
(thermo-aqueous deposits) and No. 3 (stratified doposits) are resources of speoial
importanoe. s a matter of feot, contact deposits are olosoly connooted with
thormo-aqueous ds osits, and it is often diffioult to distinguish one from the other
1. Contaot Deposits
Contact deposits are the most important type of deposit in CHInA. and
almost all big iron mines in the country belong to this type. Usually, the ere
body lies in or near the contact surfaoe of granite porphyry. limestone, and
silex, and the size of the ore bodies varies from small ones which are only
interesting from scientifio viewpoint to huge ones whioh oontain several tens of
millions of tons of minorals.
The ores are magnotite and heematite, but the latter is most abundant.
Magnetite is often found in the ore beds near to igneous rocks and is usually found
together with heematite. of the by-produots, there are sulphides like iron pyrites.
ohalcopyrite, end (斑 铜 ) and sometimes, the quality of the ore itselr is
low beoause of the large amount of by-products. For instanoe, in one of the mines
in Central CHIna, experionce in the past has proved that sulphides inorease as
mining goes deeper and deeper.

Published in TOKYO, 29 July 1944
WHAT IS EXPECTED OF THE NEW CABINET (a symposium)
Present:
ROYAMA MASAMICHI (鼠 山 政道)
KOMIG IWA RITOKU (小汀 利得）
TSUKUI TATSUO (津# 龍雄)
NAKAJ DMA YADANJI(牛島彌團次)
KONO MITSU (河 野 密)
KIYOSE ICH IRO (清 員一郎)
Representative of our agency:
ISH IBASHI TANZAN (石 橋 湛山)
ISiIBAShI: Let me say a few words. It seems that the Cabinet will
undergo some changes, and I think there must be various problems in
regard to the changes. There won't be any objection to the necessity
of powerful politics for the sake of passing through this crisis.
The subject assigned to the change of cabinet is the materialization
of powerful politics. I would like to have your frank opinions
about this. As an M.P. NAAJLMA is in a hurry, let him say something
first.
Rushed up Increase of Production First of All
NAKA IMA: As we have to win this war at all events, my earnest hope
of the new cabinet is an urgent policy for increasing the production
of arms especially aircraft. We must give up the annual plan and
concentrate our efforts at a speeded-up increase of production.
First, to take up the problem of resources, I think we must carry
out a speeded-up transportation of bauxite, the raw material of
aluminum, and of other raw materials from the Southern Regions.
Second, as we depend upon MANCHURIA and CHINA for many things, we
should revise the Plan for the Mobilization of Resources, carry out
an ahead-of-schedule transportation, and secure resources, raw
materials, and materiel. This is my greatest hope of the new cabinet.

=== Page 103 === (confidence 0.99)
CO-ORDINATED TRANSLAT ION CENTER
TR 314 - P. 2
CHUNGKING
23 FEB 1945
Renewal of Different Measures
NAKAJ MA: Next thing is the Plan for the Mobilization of Labor.
Since the execution of the adjustment of industries, there has still
been much unused labor in the cities. These men are so-called 
by-standers in war time, and I wish the government would exert its
efforts to wipe out this aspect. As to schools, only the primary
schools () should be ma intained; the students of
other schools can all be used as laborers. Besides, I think we
must make a plan for introducing large numbers of Koreans and Chinese
The Plan for the Vobilization of Resources is divided into
three parts. I think we should simplify it and make it adaptable,
uniform, and moreover, flexible. This should be done for the uti-
lization of resources; otherwise, our fighting power will not be
well developed.
oreover, there are many secret matters concerning our supreme
command. I think there are two reasons for this. One is the pre-
vention of spies, in other words, for strategical purposes, and the
other, scientific technique, or manufacturing industries. One is
spiritual, and the other, material. Thus, the secret matters con-
cerning our supreme command are numerous. If the government would
reduce secret matters, and cooperate more with the people, our
fighting power would be increased a good deal. I also hope the
government will take some measures concerning the release of war
news. I have held symposiums aimost every night with the people of
the electoral districts and asked them about many things, but they
were all afraid to speak. Freedom of speech has been denied. This
is certainly not a proper way to stimulate their will to fight.
The people are so bacly restricted by the so-called Special Penal
Law of War Time and the National Security Law that they have become
as timid as the people in the feudalistic period. Therefore, the
fighting power which could have been developed remains undeveloped
now. I don't think we can attain a sure victory unless the govern-
ment will reduce the secret matters of our supreme command, let
the people know more, share its worry with them, and cooperate
with them.
The Necessity of a People's Organization
NAld DiA: Another thing is that we have no organization of the peopl
to cope with the great war. There are the Imperial Rule Assistance
Society and the Youth Corps for the Assistance of the Imperial Rule,
but they were not formed by the people of their own will. Even
the Administrative Council (  ) is not well united. It
consists of people of various circles including members of Parliament,
but they do not cooperate. This is because the organization has
no limbs. There is no subsidiary association to the Council. The

=== Page 104 === (confidence 0.99)
CO-ORDINATED TRANSLATION CENTER
TR 314 - P. 3
CHUNGKING
23 FEB 1945
Imperial Rule Assistance Society has limbs, that is to say, it has
subsidiary associations, and its work is carried out thoroughly by
the prefectural governors, but it has no body and no head. The
head hes vanished in the realm of the government officials, and is
separated from the l imbs. I was a member of one of the old political
parties. I am not advocating the revival of political parties,
but I don't think we can get along in the present situation if we
have no union of comrades. For this reason, I hope the government
will give more freedom and be more generous in acknowledging poli-
tical associations. The people organize political associations
for the sake of winning the war but never for the purpose of losing
the war. Therefore, it is necessary for the government to acknow-
ledge political associations and let the people express their frank
opinions. The more political associations are formed, the more will
the people's organizations be developed and strengthened, I think.
Many organizations were adjusted and have been united into the
Imperial Rule Assistance Society. Seventy years have elapsed since
JAPA got rid of feudalism, but wherever you go, you still find
strong provineial feeling. This is the time when we must concentrate
the total power of the people in fighting the war, but the organiza-
tion of the Administration Council and the Imperial Rule Assistance
Society is merely a formal one; it does not permeate to the lower
levels. Therefore, it is necessary to investigate all over again
the people's organizations in both the central district and the
provinces; but first of all, the government must acknowledge poli-
tical associations.
Next, I hope the government will establish a system of sure
victory by allowing the Diet to manage things. The Tapanese Diet
has died since the outbreak of the CIVA Incident. Recently, it
has revived a little, but even in a session of the Diet, we don't
have a report on the war situation in detail. The people's will
to fight will never be stimulated under such conditions. These
must be thoroughly reformed. The government must guide the people
through the Diet, and the members of Parliament should be good
patriotic fighters and should also move in the direction of bettering
the government.
A Suggestion for Prevention of Inflation
Nutad Ii: The last thing I want to mention is the policy for a
thorough prevention of malignant inflation. What is the fundamental
reason for the rampancy of black market where prices are 10 or 20
times higher than the present official prices? It is the weakness
of political power. Ve must strengthen our political power. As to
the drawing up of the budget, the special war expenses, which are
about 80% of the total budget, and the budget for the demands of
the people are all drawn up with secret figures. We must break the
secrecy of the budget and remove the cause of malignant inflation
which is virtually the budget itself. We are spending a huge amount
of money in seven-years' plans and five-years' plans, but this is

=== Page 105 === (confidence 0.99)
CO-ORD INATED TRANSLATION CENTER
TR 314 - P. 4
CHUNGKING
23 FEB 1945
not the time for such plans. Even in GERMANY and ENGLAND, work
which takes more than two years to accomplish is being carried out
ahead of schedule. Besides, much of the principle of a peacegtime
balanced budget, the principle of participation, (分  主 )
and the principle of local distribution still exist. I think We
ought to change them to war time principles and try not to spend
money to no purpose. Moreover, we must think over matters like
the simplification of issuing orders, the adjustment of the indus-
trial system, and the like.
To take a further step, we should adopt the policy of curreney
absorption. For this purpose, the system of taxation must be funda-
mentally reconstructed. At present, the income of the people is
flowing into the so-called newly rich class. Laborers, especially
laborers in military plants, farmers who live in the neighborhood
of cities, repair men, those who have smuggled money from SHAnGHAI,
and particularly the day 1aborers, are making 30 to 40 Yen a day.
The system of taxation on these people has not been established
yet, and we hope it will be establ ished in one way or other. We
can't collect taxes from them with the present loose system of
taxation. T am thinking of a system of taxation according to pro-
fessions in regard to this. I don't want to tell you the details,
but I think the system of property taxation should also be put
into practice. Now, income has been mobilized, but not property.
If you were not clear about property, you would not know how the
property of the nation could become the property and income of the
people, so I hope this will be carried out at any rate.
Tere is another thing which also has something to do with my
own principle. le have a lot of government property, which is only
next to that of SOV IET .JSS14 in abundance. There must be 30, O00,
000,000 Yen to 50,000,000,000 Yen. This can be shifted from the
shoulders of the government to those of the people. I think this
is also a method of absorbing currency.
Establishing a Thorough-going System
KIYOsE: As the TOJO cabinet resigned for the sake of awakening the
people from langor, I don't think it is enough to let KOIsO take
the place of TOJO. It wil1 make no sense if JAPAI does not éstablish
a final and thorough-going system of taxation, both at home and
abroad. It is a regrettable matter that we have no more elder
statesmen of the MiIoI era who were popular among the people, but we
can still have an all-star cast consisting of the whole group of
our senior statesmen.
Another thing is that we should freely make those closely con-
nected with the people members of the cabinet. We may well resume
the old system of assigning executive officers. Particularly in
the Army and the Navy, we must assign executive officers, for we can
see the reflection of the secrets thoughts of the people in the
mirror of the executive officers. I think they are necessary for
a long-term war. .

=== Page 106 === (confidence 0.99)
CO-ORDINATED TRANSLAT ION CENTER
Tn 314 - P. 5
CHUNGKING
23 FEB 1945
Another point is that modern war is very much a war of thought
and stratagem. Therefore, the matter-of-fact Bureau of Intelligence
can never cope with the war. Even the principal of a middle school
can understand what has been written above. The government should
assign a General Staff of thought or a minister of propaganda to
take up the responsibility of direct assistance. ....
Nothing but Great Increase of Production
KIYOSE: I have only one hope of the new cabinet. That is what Mr.
NAKAJ IMA has just said, a definite increase of the arms and munitions
on which we must stake everything. By increase of production, I
don't mean an increase of 10% or 20%. That's useless. To consider
aircraft alone, we won't be able to win the war unless we can
increase the present production by (blank) times. If the government
can only succeed in this, I won't blame it, no matter what failures
it may make. That is the only way to save our country. Fortunately,
we have enough raw materials to serve our purpose. There are 1imi-
tations in iron ore resources, and we cannot get as much as we wish
to, but we have more bauxite and bauxite shale, the raw material for
aluminum, than AlilnICh. In spite of this fact, our production is
only (blank)% of that of AlimICA. I doubt whether this should
happen in war time or not. The matters of finance and thought which
Mr. NAKAc EfA has mentioned are measures for promoting the increase
of production, but high morale is necessary Tor this. Therefore,
the best way is tc let the people know the real war situation and
make them feel the absolute necessity of increasing production. Now,
as the competition in production between JAPAN and AiMERICA is a
war, I think those people who obstruct the increase of production
should be considered guilty of hindering production and should even
be shct. If the government coula greatly inerease the production
of munitions, especially of aircraft, all other failures would be
excusable. It won't matter even if there is an inflation or shortage
of food, or the loss of the chance for children to go to schools.
Wishing for a Convincing Politics
TSJKUI: I have a hope not only of the new cabinet but of all cabinets.
In short, I hope the government will carry out a kind of politics
which can convince us. Politics which are not convincing can never
strengthen our feeling of sacrifice and service even if marvelous
slogans are made public and strong power exercised. By convincing
politics, I mean administration with thought as its foundation. You
may take thought as a very abstract thing, but it is linked with
many realistic spheres. Especially, it is closely connected with
politics. It seems that there has been an inclination to guide the
people's thought by a narrow and prejudiced concept. If we go to
extremes with such a concept, we may come to a conclusion that a
greater part of our people are unworthy people. When such a thought
is linked with realistic politics, the political tone will become
low, and it will arouse a very dark feeling among the people.

=== Page 107 === (confidence 0.98)

CHUNGKING
23 FEB 1945
Therefore, I hope the government will guide the people with a more
far-reaching, ORAku(?)(才   力) deep, and bright thought. It
is the same with the peoples of GREATER EAST ASIA. Furthermore,
I wonder if probably SoV IET RUSSIA is not a country on whom there
is still room left for us to employ diplomatic processes for the
sake of winning the war; and in such a case, communism will nece-
ssarily become a problem. If JAPAN has the right idea, she will
never follow communism blindly, nor will she oppose it simply by
impulse. She will be able to handle communism or, to take a further
step, assimilate it with Japanese thought. In this sense, thought
is necessary for a realistic and active diplomacy. A few days ago,
the government made a statement concerning the continental operations.
It seemed that nothing was mentioned about the problem of the commu-
nist party in H IvA. Mevertheless, we had an impression that some
active measures might be taken with regard to this problem. There-
fore, we can see that even in the case of Ci INA, there is the pro-
blem of communism.
In short, we need convinc ing politics or politics closely con-
nepted with the peonle; and in their foundation, a deep and Okaku(?)
(才   力 ) thount is necessary, I think.
Rich Thought Please
SUGLiOnI; I listened gratefully to what Mr. NAKAJDiA has just said.
I think he has mentioned the chief points of our expectations of
Japanese politics as well as of the government. Mr. TSUKUI spoke
on thought especially. I also think thought is necessary for us
to fight the war, to win it, in other words, to form the GREAT EAST
ASIA CO POSPEnITY PT, and to establish the NEW WORLD ORDER.
I have always bel ieved that it is too bad to have the thought remain
so incomplete. Mr. TsJui also talked about diplomacy. It makes
no difference whether our object is RUSSIA or not. As a matter of
principle, thought in politics must be fully developed when we deal
with ail foreign countries. This is necessary for the substance
and quality of our diplomacy rather than for propaganda and stratagem.
As subjects of JAPAN, we must consider seriously whether or
not our country has a way of thought to be thoroughly advocated
both at home and abroad. From this standpoint, we can find an
example in what is usually called modern thought which all the
peoples of GhEaTEn EAsT ASIA and other foreign countries can manage
to understand. This is the fimdamental thought which can be imme-
diately materialized in the best way. In the first place, it is
the principle of science. We draw other peoples toward us by science.
We will have, as our objects, not only material things but also
spiritual and social things. It is absolutely necessary for us to
understand our enemy AvEnICA, to handle USSIA from our standpoint,
and to deal with the different peoples of GREATER EAST ASIA. I
think such a thought must occupy a remarkable position in the in-
ventive power and motive power of our people.

Observation of the Total War Politics
RoYAMA Now, as the TOJO cabinet has resigned, OYAt I think it is
necessary for us to observe seriously the problem of politics in
total war.
In order to get materials for studying this problem, I am reading
books written by statesmen of different countries during the First
WORLD WAh. In the records of the Great War written by CHURCHILL,
it is mentioned that at the end of the last WORLD WAR, the British
people haraly thought of victory or defeat but advanced straight
toward the prosecution of War. Therefore, when the trumpet of the
armistice was sounded; they did not even know which side had won
the War.
In the middle of the war, there was a lot of argument about the
politics of total war in BRiTAL, and there were also various changes
in political circles. However, as the result of inventive devices,
political activities were centralized and political maneuvers and
strategy were also harmonized in the latter part of the war. I
imagine by then, the British people paid no more attention to such
problems but were in a frame of mind to advance in the war with every
effort. Here, we can see the general direction of politics in total
war. However, as we Japanese are inexperienced in the politics of
total war we must study the way of harmoniz ing and adapting certain
points to the circumstances of our country. After all, I think the
principle'of the fundamental policy lies in the statements JAPAN
has made since the outbreak of the EATEn EAST ASIA WAR. We may
call it the idea of HArkOICIU", (  - 宇 - the universal
control) in other words, letting all peoples have their right spheres
of action, and allow ing them to live in peace. I think this idea
is probably the chief point of our politics in total war. We have
already applied this principle in the GhEATEn EASI ASIA CO-PROSPERITY
SPdinE. I think we can also apply it in our cwn country.
The Necessity of Encouraging the People to Take up Their
Responsibilities
OYAMA: However, according to our experience since the CHINA Incident
especially the expericnce of the two and a half years since the
outbreak of the ATTn TAST ASIA WA, politics in total war has
ended controlled economy and controlled politics. It is narrow-
minded to ccnsider the politics of total war in such a way. For
under such conditions, the government cannot let the people take
charge of what the government itself is not able to manage. The
people will not trust a government which fails to take up it respon-
sibilities, and their life will also become uneasy for that reason.
For instance, there are limitations in the power of the governmer
even in the food problem. It is necessary to let the people take
charge to some extent of matters concerning the production, distri-
bution, and consumption of food. To let the people do the job by

=== Page 109 === (confidence 0.99)
CO-ORD INATED TRANSLATION CENTER
TR 314 - P. 8
CHUNGKING
23 FEB 1945
themselves means liberalism, which is only a different expression
of my idea. It is obvioue enough that liberalism alone will not
do, but this principle is also a national discipline. Ian't it truo
that we can only develop our total power by supplementing the power
of the government with liberalism?
General Headquarters and Cabinet in Total War Politics
What about specific measures? I have not studied this question
long enough, but I think after all, it depends upon the system of
the cabinet in the politics of total war and on the relation of the
cabinet with the supreme command, namely, the General Headquarters.
Here are two specific questions about organization. One is the
construction of the cabinet itself, and the other is the connection
between the cabinet and the General Headquarters. .......
In order to apply the above mentioned principle in the present
critical situation, we must achieve two purposes: getting all men
of talent in the cabinet, and maintaining connections between the
cabinet and the Headquarters.
There are two ways of getting all men of talent. One is to ask
all the senior statesmen who can be considered as clders of the Meiji
era to enter the cabinet. However, we cannot have all the senior
statesmen serve concurrently in ali ministries. We should do better
to choose worthy young men who are really linked with the people
or hol a position in the Diet. Besides, there are other systems,
like the system oi executive officers, and I think we can apply
them properly on the same principle.
Vhat is specially important is the second question I mentioned
above, the connection between the war time cabinet and the General
Headquarters. We will not only have a joint committee of the ca-
binet and the General Headquarters; we should have a council and
make it the supreme organ. In none of the belligerent countries
can we find such a vague and divergent system as ours. In order to
take a further step, think it is absolutely necessary to have
a consulting organ, an organ to make decisions.
Let Publ ic Opinion be Active
KONO: The statement about "for the sake of awakening the people from
languor" is the best explanation of the significance of the recent
political change. I think there are various aims in awakening the
people from languor, but the most important one is freedom of speech.
In these few years, the people have not been able to say frankly
what they think. They have been afraid that they might be blamed
if they spoke about certain matters. They hesitated, and tried to
patch up the surface, so the public mind has really become timid. We
can never develop the total power of the people in this way. I think
the government has al so not iced this point and has been convinced

=== Page 110 === (confidence 0.99)
CO-ORD INATED TRANSLAT ION CENTER
TR 314 - P. 9
CHUNGKING
23 FEB 1945
that it must awaken the people from languor now. That is why there
happens to be this political change. The important thing that the
coming cabinet must consider is letting the people say what they
wish to and asking for their cooperation. The new cabinet must
make their way with such a presumpticn. Mr. KIYOSE said a little
while ago that the fundamental problem is the increase of the pro-
duction of munitions. I fully agree with him. However, when we
go around the aircraft factories and the ship yards, we can see
that everything is all right superficially, but that there is no
soul. What is the matter? In such a case, I think it is important
to make the people feel that each of them is responsible for fighting
this war. There are different methods for this, but if we don't
make the point clear, discussions and changing the cabinet will
make no sense at all. Therefore, the duty of the new cabinet is
to take drastic measures for this problem.
Another thing we ought to think about is the question of res-
ponsibility. no is going to be responsible? I am very sorry that
this has not been mude clear yet; that is to say, the allocation of
responsibility is unclear. The next cabinet should at least make
the allocation of responsibility clear. If it should ever make a
mistake in guidance, and do enything against our purpose, it should
take the blame itself. Otherwise, the people cannot foliow it
with confidence.
Specific  easurcs for Increase of Lation
Ko..fufnk: I don't think it is necessary to add anything more, but
I will say a few words about economics.
Though it is said that we are fighting a total war, the actual
condition is not so desirable. It is the same with economic affairs
as witn the political organ and the employment of politics about
which you have been talking for some time.
Let me mention a few ordinary facts which everyone sees and
experiences daily. In and around Tolo, the distribution of vege-
tables for one person is one carrot every other day. It would be
all right to ive this much to a horse as extra food, but it will
not do for a man.  ext, this is the problem of the prefectural bloc.
So long as this kind of foolish thing is going on, our total power
can never be developed, not to mention other matters. If we could
think it over without any prejudice, the matter would become very
simple, and if everyone could th ink it over, the system of total
war would be perfectly set up tomorrow. I will give a few specific
measures for improvement in simple words. Let us take the example
of vegetables. Distribution is not well done, not because we are
short of food, but because the method is bad. I will not mention
anything about the prefectural bloc and the purchases in the country
because everybody knows about them. The most serious bottleneck is
considered to be the 1 ack of transportation, but this was taken for

Fundamental Measures for Labor
Ko iIvA: Next, we have the labor problem. I have written several
times about the abolishment of conscription, urging the government
to think the matter over. As a matter of fact, we can improve it
instead of abolishin it. But, because there have been obstacles,
we must perform a rough operation by abolish ing it. Otherwise, we
cannot set up a labor system to lea us to victory. Therefore, I
advocate the abolishment of conscrintion. e must think of a way
of raising the efficiency of each laborer rather than getting large
numbers of them. The method we have been adopt ing can only shake
society, and make the people uneasy. Por instance, laborers who
could have cone a great deal for the country in their origiual fields
of work were trans ferred to fields of lower efficiency only to
reduce the national production. "oreover, this is causing disorder
in trains and busses. In one place, houses rema in vacant, wh ile
in others new houses and rooms are heeded.
What is worse is that two laborers are sent to a job where one
is enough, and three are sent to another job where two can suffice.
Therefore, the laborers tend to be lazy. Then they go to the fac-
tories to work, some of them can get rations, but some cannot. They
work quite diligently in the factories where they can get ration,
but in the factories vhere ration is not given, they will become
lazy, and will go to the country for purchasing whenever they are
free. Some of ther are of bad character, but some do so because they
have children or are in difficulty because some of their family
members are sick. This problem can be solved by improving the method
of distribution. I would like to bring forward a specific measure
now. The method of distributing articles by the government to la-
borers as their rights should be abolished, and the government should
give the articles to factory directors, managing directors, or company
managers. Anyway, it should give the articles to the factories. The
companies should hand the articles to laborers of high efficiency,
who are dil igent and hard-working men. yothing should be given to
the lazy ones. If this method could be put into practice, the la-
borers would certainly work hard. Then there is no necessity for the
honest workers to go to the country for purchasing, efficiency will
rise also.

Key Point of Problem
KOIGIwA: Here is another thing which has something to do with the
problem of thought and with other problems. Recently, they have
been reducing the amount of wine made. They are going to reduce
it this year too. They are also going to cut down tobacco manu
facture. But it makes me furious because those who advocate the
cutt ing down of wine making and tobacco manufacture are drinking and
smoking. I don't want to boast, but neither do I drink nor smoke
because I think wine and tobacco are harmful. If drinking and
smoking could ever be prohibited, I would be no less enthusiastic
than others for abst inence. However, at the present time, there is
no room for us to carry out such things as the probibition of to-
bacco or wine. We are in the midst of a great war which is to
decide our destiny. Tow, we ought to concentrate our attent ion on
producing one more weapon or one more shell, in rais ing efficiency,
and in mak ing the people fight more OrakA(?) (才 木  力). It is
no t ime to think of the cutting down of wine making and tobacco
manufacture alone. If we coulā make 1,000,000 koku (石 - 39,7033
gallows) more of wine, there would certainly be a great difference
in the efficiency o  apanese industry. It is impracticable to say
that food in JaPa will become scarce if much wine is made. I think
the government must not be so stingy as to permit only a certain
number or persons to buy wine, and i init the distribution of wine
for each fanily to (bl ank) so (合 - 0.318 pint) per month. It must
tell the people not to worry, and that enough wine will be supplied
to them. Then, no man will leave his work at three o'clock to go to
a bar.
Working from  conon ic Ideas
some people say that tobacco fields should be reduced some
40,000 cho, (J - .45 acres) but the 1ength of time needed for
growing tobacco exclusively is 80 days at most, sometimes only 20
days will be enough. Therefore, it is an impracticable view of
those who don't understand economic matters to say that growing to-
bacco will reduce the production of fooa. If we express it in an
exaggerated way, some Fields are free from insects because of growing
tobaccc. It really belps to increase the production of food as an
insecticide. ecently, some unreasonable persons have been inclined
to interrere with ot her people's fields while overlooking their own
fields.  think we must correct these points with the clever judge-
ment of a man cf the world.
I can tell you another example of the waste of extremely im-
portant war resources of the nation. lor instance, there was a
"safety week" some time ago. It was not a bad th ing but a good one.
When we rode on a tra in car, we used to see around 10 men wearing
around their arms a piece of cloth,

einvestigation of the Problem of Prices
Ko. liIv4: Another point is the problem of commodity prices on

other day. Por instance, it (the alandonment of official prices)

The resources of APA are poor, but they are not so poor as
to make the war situation as unfaverable as it is now. Neither our
production nor our labor is so weak as te make the war situation as
bad as it is now. I believe things will become better if we go
on in the right way.
I hope the new cabinet will boldly put into practice the above
mentioned points, ana it is unnecessary to add that the cabinet
must have a streng political power and be able to beat any force

THE LOBILIATION OF SPUDENTS AID PUPILS.
Labor is the ssence of ducation:
Question:
What are the reasons behind the "Student
Labor decree"?
Answer: The procedure of mobilization and the employment
of student labor 1: based upon the act of National Labor
mobilizatlon. In the very begin ing, the act of National
Labor obllization was formulated on the principles of
National obilization It aims to mobilize the whole popu-
latlon to utilise their loisure time for war productlon and
other important military purposes. In essence this is
different from the present student mobilization.
This does not mean that the students have to sacrifice
their lear In temporarily and devote their time for labor
service, but on the contrary, the studonts have to achieve
the aim of education through working. hat is to ay, the
student: cet to offer their services to the country in the
form Labor 1: education( 勤 即 是教育) hence this
i ot e te porary affair, but yoar around plan. Therefore,
the procedures and regulation: had to be drafted in this
spirit.
In order to meet the specific requirements of the
anmunition factories and at the sare time to enable the
carryin out of  obilization smoothly 1t is nece: sary to
separate student labor obilization from the eneral labor
mobilization hence the " tudent Labor  obilization decree"
was issuéd.
uestion: "Labor Is educatlon" i: very i portant, but
Answor: The phrase "Labor is education" is often
misunderstood to mean teaching the laborers in their leisure
hours; this would naturally lead the stud nts to think that
they aro treated as apprentices. This 1s certainly not the
right interpretation. It moans to the teacher that educa-
tion of the student is possible only through labor, and to
the student it means celf-training, learning and self-disci-
pline throh work.
The employers ust treat ther as students and they must
welcome them with a warm heart and honest enthuslaer. They
have to look after their health and hyglenic conditions, and

=== Page 141 === (confidence 0.97)
CO-ORDINATED TRANSLATION CENTER
Th 307 - page. 9
CHUNGKING
21 FEB 1945
to guide their thinking so as to make the student work dill-
gently in a friendly atnosphere. This kind of cooperatlon
Is compatible with "Labor is educatfon" and will reatly
stimulate the efficiency of production.
The worst possible arrangement 1s to treat the students
as ordinary laborers and try to exploit the as much as pos-
sible. The other extrene 1: to take as many students as
possible and ive them unimportant jobs or even no work to
do and lot thom loaf around.
If on the oe hand the employers respect the personality
of the stude ts and take pains to explain to the their diffi-
cultius, and on the other hand praise ther for their good
work, whe there Is an inereaso In production, then through
this attitude of sharing bitternes: and happiness together
the e otional and rateful :tudent: can be moved to work
very hard for the factory, regardless of nersonal sacrifices.
we therefore, hope the employers will understand the psy-
choloay of the  tudonts and guide then to creative and pro-
ductive work.

direotly wdth the Manohurian Heavy Industry Co. Consequently. the new tendenoy

Mr. Paul H. Johnstone, in charge of Japanese work,

Enemy Branch, FEA

ILH/Mr. Plank
1—1493
At Mr. Johnstone's suggestion I had lunch with him
and discussed economic work which is being done in FEA
looking to the post-hostilities treatment of Japan. He
explained that he has a division of eighty-odd employes
working on Japan and that, among other things, they are
studying the iron and steel industry and population trends.
He went on to say that they were experiencing some diffi-
culties in making policy assumptions upon which to base
their work and, therefore, had undertaken to draft a memo-
CS
randum on basic assumptions which they would like to dis-
cuss with interested officers of this Department at some
future date.
WILED
I told Mr. Johnstone about the Interdivisional Eco-
nomic Committee on Japan and our hopes of appointing a
coordinator for economic work on Japan. I said that we
would be glad to discuss with him problems of mutual interest
2
and that the Interdivisional Committee has under consider-
ation an agenda of background economic studies which members
of the Committee will probably want to discuss with him in
the near future.

-3/45

=== Page 186 === (confidence 0.98)
ARD FORM NO. 64
OFD: - Mr. Collado
A-C: - Mr. Orchard
DATE: Apr11 24, 1945
TO
tCC
ED: - Mr. Lockhart
FROM
SUBJECT:
Comments of Secretary's Staff Committee on SC-lOl:
Economic Policy toward Japan
As reported in the minutes of yesterday's meeting, Mr.
894.50/4-2445
Grew's views appear to be generally in line with the position
taken in the paper. However, he would require Japanese labor
to do reconstruction work, especially in China. The reasons
for doubting the wisdom of this proposal are stated in the
middle paragraph of page 13 of the Coordinating Committee's
draft (CC-45).
Mr. Acheson commented that the paper was very poor and
full of both meaningless and dangerous platitudes.  It is
difficult to know precisely what matters Mr. Acheson had in
mind but it would seem that he is not fully aware of the fact
that the paper was prepared at the request of and along the
lines indicated by the Executive Committee on Economic Foreign
Policy. That Committee desired an outline of the longer range
overall economic policy toward Japan within the framework of
the general objective of enduring peace. It is probably true
that any statement of general foreign policy is in danger of
sounding platitudinous, but as Mr. Grew pointed out, and as
indicated on page 2 of the paper, there are too many imponder-
ables to spell out recommendations in detail. That cannot
safely be done until we have ascertained actual conditions
in Japan at the time of surrender. Consequently, the paper
does not attempt to draw a detailed blueprint of the postwar
economy of Japan or to list specific industries and plants
for destruction or retention. It is believed that this general
and provisional statement of economic policy in the Far East,
particularly toward Japan, provides a better basis for the
development of a common economic policy in that area among the
United Nations than a more detailed statement could provide.
