=== Page 192 === (confidence 0.99)
April 29, 1945.
Memorandum to Mr. Collado
From: O. C. Lockhart
Re: SC-lOl: Economic Policy toward Japan
Our discussion yesterday helped, I think, to clarify Mr. Acheson's
approach to the paper and consequently to explain the basis of his strictures
on it. Since it appears that the Staff Committee does not expect the drafting
officer to explain his paper in person, I suggest that a statement such as that
appended should help to obviate Mr. Acheson's objections.
In considering this paper, it is necessary first of all to have in mind
the differences and the relationships existing between a statement of long-run
policy and a directive.
A statement of policy is a broad outline or plan of action for the
attainment of certain objectives; it is directed toward ultimate ends, such as
"enduring peace." A directive, on the other hand, is an instruction or a series
of instructions relating to specific actions to be taken or omitted; it is con-
cerned with immediate and limited objectives which are, nevertheless, believed
to be essential to the creation of conditions that will permit the attainment.
of the ultimate objectioes of policy. A statement of policy thus provides the
foundation or frame of reference for a directiwe. The difference between the
two is somewhat analogous to that between strategy and tactics.
The paper on Economic Policy toward Japan is such a statement on longer-
run policy. As such it does not and should not attempt to provide a detailed
blue print of Japan's postwar economy. It concerns itself primarily with ulti-
mate objectives rather then with the specific means of achieving them; since
those means will be clearer when conditions in post-surrender Japan are defin-
itely known. The paper is to be distinguished, for example, from such a paper
as the directive to the commander in the European theater, which concerns iteelf
with immediate objectives centering largely in the security of the occupying
forces in the initial post-defeat period. The directive emphasizes the fact
that it is not an ultimate statement of policies concerning treatment in the
postwar, and accordingly provides for constant surveys of conditions as a basis
for supplemental directives.
What the paper on longer-run Economic Policy toward Japan attempts to
do, then, id to provide a framework of general policy objectives within which
may be fitted papers that may be required on more specific aspects of the post-
war treatment of Japan, including directives to the theater commander. In the
course of its drafting and passage up to the Secretary's Staff Committee, it
has had the benefit of comment by a considerable mumber of officere of the
Department and a gratifying degree of unarvimity of opinion as emerged. Its
statements of policy have been fotlowed, so far as applicable, in the prepar-
ation of a momorandum for the President which, if approved, will be the basis
for the directive for military government in Japan; and it is expected that it
will also guide in appropriate measure the work of the Adviser on Japanese
Economic Affairs and the Committee on Economic Studies on Japan.

SECRET
Draft No. 12-A
25 April 1945
SUMMARY OF U.S. ECONOMIC POLICY IN THE INITIAL
POST-DEFEAT PERIOD RELATING TO JAPAN
I. Induotrial Divarmament and Demilitarization
Military government shall enforce a program of industrial
disarmament and demilitarization with the aim of preventing
Japan from again developing an economic war potential. Japan
shall be permitted to retain no specialized facilities for
the production or repair of implements of war or aircraft of
any type. Except in emergency situations there should be no
destruction of facilities readily convertible to civilian
production, pending decision on disposition either by transfer
abroad or use in Japan.
II. Control of Japanese Economy
In order to meet the needs of the occupation forces and
to prevent starvation and such disease and civil unrest as
would interfere with the operations of military government,
military government shall take such steps as may be necessary
with respect to (a) essential national public services;
(b) finance and banking; (c) production and distribution of
essential commodities; and (d) exports and imports. No steps
shall be taken by the military government which would provide
a standard of living to the Japanese people out of line with
that of neighboring peoples.
III. Reparations, Restitution, and Relief
In accordance with decisions by the、appropriate authorities
military government shall arrange for relief to the United
Nations and to liberated areas, for restitution of identifiable
looted property, and for reparations. The first charge on the
proceeds of allrexports shall be a sum necessary to pay for
approved imports. these claims taking precedence over repara-
tions elalns. Military government shall not for reparations
or relief purposes assist in the restoration or development
of Japanese heavy industries which might contribute to the
rearmament of Japan or whose expansion might promote dependence
by other countries on Japan for strategic products.
IV. Promotion of Democratic Forces
Military government shall encourage the development of
democratic organizations in labor, industry, and agriculture,
and shall favor the widedistribution of ownership, management
and control of the Japanese economic system.
a
V. Control
SECRET

=== Page 199 === (confidence 0.98)
SECRET
-2-
V. Control of Foreign Credit
No credit shall be extended Japan or any Japanese
person by foreign persons or governments, except with
approval of military government.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Memorandum of Conversation
OFFICE OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
April 14, 1945
DATE: May 24, 1945
SUBJECT:
Japenese Fost-war Problems
PARTiciPAnTs: Ikuo Oyama, Northwestern University, Evanston, Illinois.
John K. Emmerson
894.50/5
COPIES TO:
5-2445
1-1493
Professor Oyama is probably the most outstanding Japa-
①
nese individual now living in the United States. He was
at one time head of the Farner Labor Party in Japan and was
elected to the Diet in 1930. He came to the United States
in 1932 and has been living in retirement in Evanston since
that time. He has done some teaching and research at North-
western and is now enga ed in the translation of Minobe's
Constitutional Government. There is ettoched a biographical
S
sketch of Oyama translated from a Japanese book entitled
W o's Who of Proletarian Parties (Musanto So-shiden) published
in Tokyo in 1931.
0
Mr. Oyama is somewhat Leftist in his political beliefs
but he is not a Communist and, according to his statement,
894.
was attacked by the Communist International at its Sixth
Congress as one of the Social Democrats who were"hand-
maidens"of cepitalist imperialists. He is intensely in-
terested in the activities of Okano and the Japanese
55
Communists in Yenan but does not wish to make any public
statements or to affiliate himself with any Japanese groups
at the present time. His reasons re, first, he does not
wish to jeopardize the lives end security of his many fof-
lowers in Japan and second, he wishes to await a clearer
definition of United Nations policy toward Japan before
taking a stand.
1445
5
With

=== Page 205 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
With regard to post-war Japan, Mr. Oyama thinks that
unless the United Nations reestablish the finance capitalists
in power in Japan a powerful peonle's movement will gradually
develop. He does not trink that Communism will prevail in
Japan in the immediate years following the end of the war.
owever, he thinks it very possible that Japan might become
Communist within forty or fifty years.
Mr. Oyama does not think the emperor cult to be so
strongly entrenched in Japan that it can never change its
form and substance. He points out that emperor worship
has undergone various changes and has responded to outside
influences, such as Chinese philosophy. He thinks it not
improbable that after defeat the Japanese themselves, if
iven the opportunity, mey reform the emperor institution.
The constitution of Janan must of course be revised,
according to Mr. Oyama. He favors the establishment of
party government and freedom of speech and of the press.
He hopes that his translation of Minobe's work may con-
tribute to understanding of the Japanese system and the
necessary reforms which must be made in the Japanese Govern-
ment.
Mr. Oyama believes that Japanese militarism must be
completely destroyed but he feels that Japan should be
given some chance to develop peacetime industry to maintain
the livelihood of her people. He fears a resurgence of
British, Dutch, French and American imperialism in the Far
East. He expressed repeatedly his apprehension that Prime
Minister Churchill would insist that the Japanese capitalists
be supported and that colonial peoples in Asia not be per-
mitted to develop self-government. Oyama deplores a sub-
stitution of Anglo-American imperialism for Japanese
imperialism.
In spite of th domination of the militarists, Mr. Oyama
believes that there still remain strong democratic traditions
in Japan. He feels that Americans exaggerate the feelings
wrich the Japanese hold for the emperor. He mentioned that
particularly among the working classes the emperor was
often referred to with great irreverence. Oyama does not
advise that the United Nations remove the emperor but he
does believe that with free access to world information the
Japanese may themselves work out a more democratic system
of government. He pointed out that he differed fundamentally
from the Communists who advocate a one-party state and do
not permit free speech and a free press.
The most

=== Page 206 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
The most important factor in the post-war world will
be Russian-American relations. Mr. Oyama hopes that
Russia may play the role of mediator between Japan and
the United Nations. He believes that the Russian influence
upon the British and Americans might be a healthy one. He
does not believe that Russia will seize Manchuria.
Mr. Oyama says that he is ashamed of the soft, com-
fortable life he has been leading in the United States. He
is very grateful for the security which he has enjoyed here
but he says that he still has his life to give to Japan. At
the present time he conceives of his personal role as one to
be performed in the transitional period in Japan. If he
becomes convinced that conditions are favorable he would
like to return to Japan to assume the leadership of those
elements which supported him in the past. He foresees
chaotic conditions in Japan but would like to play a part
in establishing a people's movement in that country.
FE:JKEAmerson:MHP

=== Page 207 === (confidence 0.99)
IKUO OYAMA
(Chairman of the Executive Committee of Ronoto
[Peasants' and Workers' Party])
Born September 20, 1880 in the village of Wakasa,
Akaho district, Harima. His father was a doctor and he
was a second son. After graduating from primary school
he studied Chinese for one year at Kobe.  Then he went to
Osaka and studied German under Doctor Takuzo Yanagi. He
matriculated at the Kobe Industrial School in 1897 and
graduated in 190l. He intended to enter the Higher Bus!-
ness School but changed his plan and studied English and
political science at the Tokyo Specialists' School, a pri-
vate institution. This school became Waseda University
in the following year. At this time he changed his course
to political science and economics and graduated in 1905.
He was in the first class to graduate from Waseda University.
Among his classmates were Ryutaro Yanagi and Tokutsune
Aoyanagi.
Oyama was a brilliant and consistently outstanding
student. Upon  raduation he remained at the University as
a special research student and soon became a lecturer.
In 19ll he was sent by Waseda University to study in
the United States and he specialized in sociology and polit-
ical science at the University of Chicago. At that time
he was the only Japanese to take lecture notes in English.
He was called "Professor Oyama", and his knowledge and
character were highly esteemed by his acquaintances.
In 1913 he went to Germany and studied national 1aw
and political science at the University of Munich for
two years. After visiting the various countries of Europe,
he returned to Japan in l915 and became a professor at
Waseda University.
He worked very closely with Sakuzo Yoshino of Tokyo
Imperial University and launched out into the tide of
Japan's democratic era. He nurtured the growth of the
early democratic expression, rung in the new era, and
became famous as "Oyama of Wasdea". During the 1917
Reform Movement at Waseda, he left the University and
became an editorial writer for the Osaka Mainichi. During
the following year he opposed the sending of troops to
Siberia with Torii, Motogawe, Nagatani, Kawakuwa and
Korehima. The consequent pressure brought upon the Osaka
Mainichi by the Terauchi Cabinet forced him to resign his
post on the paper.
He then published the magazine Warera (WE) with
Nagatani,

=== Page 208 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
Nagatani, Kawakuwa, and Korehima and took a firm stand on
social criticism. He was called back to Waseda University
by President Takata. He again became lecturer in 1923
and during this year the Waseda military Research Group in-
cident occurred. The outbreak of the incident actually
started on May 1o when the inaugural meeting of the Mili-
tary Research Group took place in the lecture hell. Among
those present were 20 odd military personnel including
Assistant War Minister Shirakawa (then acting War Minister),
Nakajima and Konoe. They brought movie cameras to take
happenings of the ceremony.
The progressive students who were angered stated: "We
will not allow Wasede to be the propaganda instrument of
the Militarists." A group led by the Cultural League
rushed the conference hall and started the riot. On the
12th a mass meeting of students opposing militarism was
held on the campus under the auspices of the Oratorical
Club. The reactionary students who belonged to the various
clubs violently attacked the Oratorical Club members and
blood flowed.
The Military Research Group was dissolved but relations
between the opposing groups went from bad to worse. The re-
actionary force ruled the campus. Oyama was then head of the
Cultural League and various threatening leaflets were pasted
on his house. The "surrounded" group stopped going out
and even refrained from going to classes. Oyama stated:
"We cannot be absent from the University at a time like
this", and courting danger, he went to cless every day.
The Incident continued and on June 26, the Meeting
for Protecting the University was held at the Kamida Young
Men's Hall. Oyama spoke at this meeting and uttered his
well-known phrase: "Science is the weapon of the people."
Previous to this Oyama had been reserved and reluctant
to speak before a crowd. He was looked upon as an
academician and a lone democrat. However, standing up
against the oppression of the police, militarists and the
violent reactionary students, he projected himself for the
first time as an outspoken champion of the peonle. Re-
actionaries were powerful during this period, and on the
morning of the day Oyama gave his speech, Takao was killed
by Komemura, head of the Anti-Bolshevik Society.
After the outbreak of the Incident, the authorities
of Waseda stopped all lectures and controlled all speech.
President Takada had gone to Kokufu Shin, a resort, to get
away from the Incident which troubled him deeply. Oyama
decided

=== Page 209 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
decided to give lectures. He was prepared for any con-
sequence and was ready to leave the University at any time.
He sent two students to Kokufushin to notify President
Takada that he wa: going to lecture, and told the students
not to wait for his answer. After more than 5o days the
Military Research Group incident ended with the meeting
for protecting the University. The enti-militarists won
and Cyama held his position at the University.
The Kanto earthquake disaster occurred immediately
after this and Oyama was taken in custody by the Rakugo
Gendarmes and almost lost his life. In the autumn of the
same year he was made adviser to the Social Science Re-
search Institutes which sprung up all over the country, and
he became a leader of student movements in Japan. In 1924
he led the fight against the suppression of the Social
Science Research Institute and against military education.
During the same year he was made chairman of the executive
committee of the Political Research Institute efter the
right wing leaders withdrew as the result of the birth of
the Proletarian Party.
In October 1926 the Rodo Nomin-to split when the
right wing withdrew. He was chosen Chairman of the Central
Executive Committee at the first meeting of the reorganized
Party in December. Oyama accepted the chairmanship because
his Ronoto was not in a favorable position. In February
of the following year he left his post at Waseda. Presi-
dent Takada discouraged him from leaving the University.
To keep him at the University, a student movement continued
for tvo months. However, he opposed the "control" of
university faculty members and left the institution so that
in the future fundamental principles guaranteeing the uni-
versity faculty's participation in proletarian movement would
be established and also to stimulate the student movement to
ralize self-government.
From then on he gave his full time to broadening and
strengthening the Party throughout the nation, and carried
on a determined struggle. Even aristocratic scholars
joined in the struggle as militant fighters and Oyama
became a unique agitator. During the 1928 General Election
he became a candidate from the second district in Kagawa
Prefecture and fought against stront opposition. A riot
occurred and 600 were arrested at a sweep.
Following the March 15 incident, the Rodonominto was
ordered to dissolve. He was at Tottori, but he immediately
returned to Tokyo and formed the organization committee for
a new party. He fought strong opposition. In December, the
Nominto

=== Page 210 === (confidence 0.98)
-4-
Nominto (of the workers) held its inaugural conference but
on the second day of the conference, it and the organization
committee were dissolved. Oyama then formed the League
for Acquiring Political Freedom and desperately defended
the Leftist camp. He waited for the opportunity to hoist
the Ronoto banner. At this time he severed all connection
with the Communist party and he and two others issued the
so-called "Advocation of a New Party". He worked for ten
days during the hot summer to draw up the lengthy declara-
tion.
In November the new Ronoto was formed legally and
Oyama has been chairman of the Central Committee ever since.
In 1930 he ran as candidate in the fifth district of Tokyo
and won the election. One by one his associates have left
the Party on the question of its dissolution. It seems
that Oyama is a sad and lone figure.

DEPARTMENT E
JUN 1 5 1945
Dear Mr. Grew:
The Bureau of Applied Social Research has started a monthly "Poll
of Experts". The object of this new undertaking is to ascertain and report
to the public the conclusions of representative authorities on significant
questions in their special fields. There are many polls of public opinion;
there is need for a poll which will report the competent judgments of spe-
cial ists. It is hoped that a poll of this kind will help reduce the lag of
public thinking behind the views of the well-informed.
Each month we shall select a specific question of interest and im-
portance to the public and one upon which experts are in a position to voice
conclusions. You are one of the authorities whom we wish to include in this
month's panel dealing with postwar political changes in Japan. We are asking
you and some seventy-five other specially competent persons in this field
to answer the few questions enclosed. The members of the panel have been se-
lected on the basis of personal conferences with a number of "advisory experts"
concerned with these problems. (Since the polls will deal with quite varied
matters, an entirely different group of authorities will be asked to partici-
pate each month.)
The responses will be summarized to reflect all the views expressed
by the experts, but without indicating what any particular individuals have
answered. The reports will be released as a feature in The American Magazine,
a monthly publication of The Crowell-Collier Publishing Company. Reports
will also be sent to a mail ing list of interested and influential persons,
including the participating experts.
We hope you will share our conviction that this undertaking can
perform a valuable service in bringing the conclusions of a cross-section of
authorities to the attention of policy-makers and of the general public.
It will help us greatly if you will answer the questions and let us
have your reply within the next few days. A detailed report of the findings
will be sent you as soon as it is completed.
894.50/6-1145
Sincerely yours,
Arther Kowhouser
Arthur Kornhauser

June 23, 1945 Minutes of Committee on Eoonomic Polioy
Toward Japan
CD has several reservations and one correction to
record with respect to the minutes of the June 23, 1945
meeting of the Committee on Eeonomic Poliey toward Japan.
2a. It is felt that this paragraph does not refleet
the spirit of the presidential direetive which states
without qualirication that it shall be the polioy of
this Government to "encourage the development of demo-
cratic organizations in labor, industry and agrioulture,
and" to "favor a wider distribution of owmership, manage-
ment and control of the Japanese economic system®. The
directive contains no conditioning clause as is indieated
in the minutes that this action will be taken provided
894.50/7-745
it does not place an undue burden on military government.
It would seem more appropriate to try to adjust the
number of military government personnel to the problems
that will exist, rather than to attempt the reverse.
For this reason, on matters of vital security importance
to the United States, it is not thought advisable to
use as the criterion for action in Japan the aceidental
decision of some military commander on the number of men
to be trained by July 1945.
2b. The statement that military government has as
its primary objective the restoration and maintenance of
law and order is ascepted with considerable doubt.
Section I, paragraph 4 of Military Government and Civil
Affairs states: "The object of civil affairs control
through military government is to assist military opera-
SECR
tions, to further national policies and to fulfill the
obligations of the occupying forees under international
law.
2d. It is not thought that this paragraph represents
the concensus of the Committee. The Committee was given
no factpat information on Japanese farm tenancy and

A surrender olic for Japan--a current subject of
debato--is of roat importance at this time. It is well
to hour all sides of the question bofore reuching what we
cll hope cnd pray will be the most intelligont decision.
Lut it is nocessury to carefully sift all points and orient
thom to the probable shifts in Japanese thinking brought
ubout by the incro sing intensity of the United Hations
offensive in tno Fur East. Above all, What is needed is
the decision he it is with un urging on this mattor that
this uppoul is made.
An enulysis of Ja anese psycholocy, which is slowly
becoming ware of the obviousness of defeat, indicates that
Juptn is currontly tuinking in tomms of just what the loss
of tho wur will meun to the nation. If we accept such
cuncorn to bo roul, viich we most surely must, we can build
nd uido Jupnese t in ing toward uccepting surrender as
tho intolligent choice.
The majority of the terms of surrondor are obvious
both to ourselves und to tho Ju nese. others are of such
a doliccte nature that the solection of one which will cive
the Japnoso domestic propaganda machino a peg upon which
to oporate will in cll probability prolong the war in direct
proportion to the dogree of frustration which it will produce.
The followin briof discussion is centerod about thoso
s ecific itoms upon vhic un oxtremely cureful choice must
bo ..ae.
The first point upon which a careful policy decision

é
must be made is that of tho length of the occupation period.
Military nocossity comunds a short period of occupation, and
the Jt panese will understand this prospect. What will operate
to stiulate fichting spirit, however, is a policy of pro-
longed occupation with the obvious purpose of re-making the
Japanoso political and social system and forcing its acceptance.
That we desire a cnnnged domestic systen in Japan is not a
point which is essentially a war aim. There are in this.
c untry, however, propagandists of varied background who wish
to see this c unge brougnt ubout so badly that they would
suborainate the prospect of un carly end of tho war in order
that t ey might see their ends gained. The bogy which those
Who rue for the oliminution of various aspects of Japanese
socioty--und vho offer no rutional substitute program--use is
that Japan mignt ugin rise us  world power and as a threat
to world peace; needless to suy this fact is inconsistent
with the united  ations Charter and with world organization,
which is our only real u runteo of the end of a history of
intornational Cerossion.
In documentation of the point of not using occupation
boyond the requirements of militury necossity, one side of
the ar ument prosented by Dr. villian C. Johnstone in The
Future of Jupun muy be used:
The ideu of tuking udventage of occupation for the
purpose of brin ing about roforms has mot with strong
objections. Attepts at enforced reforms aro held to
be unworlable, with littlo chance of success. To impose
uny ulion iueus of institutions on the Japanese, it is
sserted, would not only stiffon their dosire for
revengo tnd incrouse their hatred of the United Nations,
but would lso violute the United Nutions principle of
solf-dotemmination. Occupation for this purpose has also

=== Page 240 === (confidence 0.99)
been opposed on more practical grounds. In an artiele
in Harpers Magazine for April l944, Nathaniel Peffer
asserts that there are not enough persons trained in
Japanese lan uage and customs or skilled in publio
administration to staff a long-term occupation govern-
mont for Japan.
From other United Nations come still different
objections to the proposal of occupation and administration
for the purpose of reforming Japanese ideas and institutions.
A British group states that 'There could be no surer way
of playing into tho hands of the military caste, dis-
crodited by defeat, than to corral the people of Japan
in an Anglo-Saxon kindergarten of internationalim.
Chinose, Filipinos, und representatives of other Asiatio
peoplos have objected to occupation for reform as too
closely reseabling the old pattern of western imporialism,
by which Japan migit tond to become a new dependeney of

DISCUSSION
1. The responsibility for the welfare of the
Japsnese people is placed upon the Japanese authorities.
The administration of relief, in the usual sense, is not
the funotion of the Supreme Commander and gratuitous
distribution of supplies by-the-Supreme Commander, as
direct relief, is authorized only me military necessity
may require. (Eoonomie Directive, paragraph 23).
The problem of rellef, in the mense of imports to

the general problem of supervising or direeting the
economy of Japan.
2. The central issue of relief poliey is the
extent to whieh the oecupation authorities should sponsor

Faets do not contain a positive statement on this point,
they do contain prohibitlons whioh clearly point the way
to such e statement.

paragraph l9b of the Economio Direotive:
"You

disease or eivil warest as would enfanger
the oodupying forees or interfere with mili-
tary pperations."
This does mot require importation but rather sets
the limit to which the Supreme Gommander may go. For imports
beyond this level, he must seek approval from the Jeint

graph 19d of the Egonomie Direetive.
Within this top limit how far should he go? Both
SWNCC 150/4 and the Seonomie Direetive contain atatements
which barely fall short of a positive prohibition against
any assistance. The whole.tenor of the passages quoted
from SWNCC 150/4 are that the Japanese people are rempen-

find their way out.
The Economio Direotive is more explioit. Paragraph 3

"You will not assume any responsibility for the

administers adequate cconomie and finaneial con-

Policy with Respect to Relief (Japan)
THE PROBLEM
1. To determine the policies with respect to relief
which should be followed by the United States military
government of Japan, including the level of subsistence to
be maintained, and the measures to be taken to secure and
distribute needed supplies.
FACTS BEARING CN THE PROBLEM
1. S NCc 107, aporoved by the State-War-Navy Coordi-
nating Comaittee on

Department of State by the SWicc Secretariat on
1945,
requests that the Department of State prepare a draft paper
on the above subject for submission to the SWNCC Secretary.
2. SiNCC 150, paragraph A II provides that:
"All instrumentalities which participate in the formu-
lation or consiceration of national policies shall be
suspended, pending the cheivement of the objectives
of military government, and their functions shall be
assumed by milit ry government."

United States military government of Japan in the initial
post-defeat period shall -
"In or er to meet the needs of the occupation forces
and to prevent starvation and such diseese end civil
unrest s voul interfere with the operations of
military government, ... take such steps as may be
necessary vith respect to (a) essential national
public services; (b) inance and banking; (c) pro-
duction and distribution of essential commodities;
and (a) exports and imports."
4. S'Ncc 150 provides in paragraph B II that -
"The immediate objective of military government in
Japan shall be the strict enforcement of the terms

DISCUSSION
1. Not enough is kno'n about United States interests
in or policy toward the parts of the Japanese Empire which
are to be separated from Japan to permit an adequate dis-
cussion of military government policy on relief in those
areas.
Therefore t! is paper has been confined to the problems
of the military government of the main islands of Japan.
?. Period of time to which discussion applies.
This discussion is with reference to the period begin-
ning shortly after the end of hostilities end extending for
three to six months.
As long as hostilities last, the accomplishment of the
military mission takes precedence over all other considera-
tions, and the military authorities will conduct civilian
supply onerations as best suits, in their opinion, the accom-
plishment of their mission. The prevention of disease and
unrest ill
1 enter into their calculations as only one of the
factors in their operations; once hostilities have ended,
civilian supply operations may be accorded higher priority.
The period of six months after the end of hostilities
is chosen arbitrarily as a period long enough to permit
military government to survey the circumstances with whicn
it must deel.
These circumstances cannot be known here or
now.
The eventual policies adopted will have to be suitable
to those

=== Page 272 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
-4-
to those circumstances.
The uncertainties may be summarized as follows:
A) Response of population.
1) The population may be submissive, or it may
be, irrespective of supplies, actively
hostile.
2) Government employees may be cooperative, or
obstructive, or refuse to function at all.
B) Extent of further destruction.
The ability of the
Japanese to provide for themselves will be much influenced
by the st te of therr equipment for production and distribu-
tion.
c) ih t amounts of food, clothing and other supplies
may still exist in stockpiles.
D) Depending lergely on (A) above, the duration of
military government is still to be determined, and its scope
and purposes will be much influenced by conditions as they
are found and as they develop. The longer MG lasts, or the
more it undertakes in the way of establishing economic as
well as political order, the more imported supplies will be
needed. Particularly is this the case if MG undertakes to
promote production for export, as reparations, or undertakes
long range reforms. In the absence of a determination of the
duration scope and purposes of MG in the light of circumstances
in Japan as they are found, only short range conclusions can
be rerched.

=== Page 273 === (confidence 0.98)
SECRET
-5-
3. Definition of Relief. The purposes of relief are
a) to prevent starvation and b) such disease and civil unrest
as woulc interfere with the operations of MG. Relief may be
defined as the provision of imported sunnlies to achieve
these purposes. Such supplies might be for immediate con-
sumption or miht be ecuipment and other supplies to
increuse Japonese production to a 1 vel adequate to meet the
ourpores of relief.
The e uipment most probably of immediate urgency to meet
these purposes is transportation equinment and supplies
necersary thereto. Other equipment or supplies looking to
ultim te increased domestic production, such as fertilizer,
might be considered on efficient form of relief, but only if
the responsibilities of Mg extend over  considerable period
of time. In gener 1, for the purnoses of the relatively
short perioc under consi erstion, it is to be expected that,
excent for transportation equipment, imported relief supplies
vill be, for most part, for direct c nsumption.
Hovever, the purposes of relief should be achieved as
far as possible vithout the importation of relief supplies,
through the use of domestically procuced surplies.
The
effort of
MG in th is cirection should be to encourage
domestic production and equitable distribution, through every
means at its command, includin reouisitioning and gratuitous
distribution

=== Page 274 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
SECRET
distribution where neceesary. Such gratuitous distributions
are frequently called relief, and might in these discussions
be called 'individual relief' to distinguish such operations,
which are required because of disorganization and personal
impecuniosity, from the operations of importing supplies
which are required because of a national shortage which
exists without reference to internel distribution or posses-
sion of the local currency.
4. Four separate nurposes are distinguishable in the
management of Japanese economic affairs: First, protection
of the troops in the accomplishment of their mission, through
the prevention of disease and unrest; Second, humanitarian
unwillingnees to stand idly by where there is starvation;
Third, the use of Japan as a productive mechanism n order to
obtain materials needed there or elsewhere. Ultimately, to
provide a sound economic basis for the establishment of law
and order, and of an cceptable Japanese civil government.
Although these puposes are distinguishable in theory, from
an operating or edministrative standpoint they are not clearly
separable. Cnly the first two come vithin the scope of
relief.
The third and ultimate curposes should not be con-
sidered within the scope of relief, although supplies fur-
nished with such purposes in mind would, in fact, serve the
nurvoses of relief.
Contrariwise, steps taken pursuant to
the purposes of relief, will often result in important aid
to the Japanese

=== Page 275 === (confidence 0.98)
SECRET
並7一
to the Jepanese people beyond the purposes of relief. For
example a railrond bridge repaired to permit the transporta-
tion of food, will also be able to carry trains whose move-
ment is important to Japanese reconstruction.
5. What diet will a) prevent starvation b) prevent
disease end civil unrest.
The adequacy of a diet to the above purposes depends
upon various circumstances - adequacy of shelter and cloth-
ing, the amount of activity reruired of the individual, diet
habit, as vell as the composition of the diet.
If the criterion of the diet is only its effect upon
the operations of military government, then it is necessary
to know the scope and duration of military government. If
military govennment continues for a long time and operates
extensively throughout the main islands, and undertakes long
range reorganizations reforms and reconstruction, it is clear
that something more than a minimum life-sustaining diet must
be proviced, either from local production solely or su ple-
mented by imports. If, on the other extreme, we occupy
only a few creas as bases, from Which expeditionary forays
of armed vehicles take place for the rapid destruction of
some installations, and if the whole operation takes only a
few months, a generally inaecuate diet would probably con-
stitute no interference with the operations of military
rovernment. If the o cup&tion is protracted in time and
extensive

=== Page 276 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
-8
extensive in area, subsistence at 1500-1700 calories would
probably need to be maintained, and adequate variety to
balance it be provided. The average pre-war per capita
diet was about 2230 calories.
The maximum average set for Germany is 1500. Accord-
ing to UNRRA studies, Chinese production probably exceeds
?500 calories average per capita, but it is not well
balanced.
In terms of calories, it is believed that about 1200
calories, as average for a whole population, is adeguate
to prevent starvation. At this level there would be sus-
ceptibility to isesse but not starvation.
Japanese domestic production during recent years has
aver ged something better than 17oo calories. War damage
might reduce production somewhat, but more important
difficulties are the probable damage to mills, difficulty
of transportation end difficulty of actual procurement.
The 17 0 calories, more or less, evailable from
normal production was short of oils and proteins. To
maintain a diet satisfactory over a longer period would
recuire increase of the fish catch and probably imports
up to 500, 000 tons of soybeans.
6. Possible inconsistency of principles lsid down.
In statements of purposes or guiding principles vith respect
to Japan, it appears that some may prove incompatible
with

=== Page 277 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
-9
with others.
This should be recognized. As an example, it
is set forth - not as clearly as might be - that the Japanese
standard of living shall not exceed that of neighboring
peoples. What does MG do then if - to prevent disease and
civil unrest - it is necessary, in their opinion, to raise
the Japanese standard of living above that of neighboring
peoples? Or, it is proposed thet allied and friendly coun-
tries have priority over scarce supplies - soybeans, sugar,
transportation equipment, textiles. Suppose, which is
likely, that the maintenance of order in Japan requires
transportation equipment that the Chinese too require. Or
that rice is not obtainable from Japanese farmers for
distribution in the cities without the use of incentive
goods such as sugar or textiles w ich are everywhere short.
During hostilities military nurnoses clearly come first; but
after hostilities, NG vill need to have some order of
priority established for the principles which may proVe in-
consistent with each other in the circumstances as they
develop.
During the post-hostilities period under reference it
is recoimended, partly for domestic political reasons, that
the ruling rineiples shall be that the standard of living
of allied and friendly liberated peoples is to exceed that
of the Jananese on the average, and that Japanese requirements
that are

=== Page 278 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
-10-
that are in short supply shall be junior in priority to all
other recuirements.
7. Measures to be taken to secure and distribute needed
supplies.
A. Primary responsibility for securing and distributing
neeccd supp'ies should rest on the Japanese themselves.
When the main islands can be dealt with as a whole,
local supplies should be adequate in amount to prevent starva-
tion, if the sunpliec can be procured snd moved from where
they are to where they are needed. It is probable that trans-
portation facilities and possibly salt will have to be
provided.
To prevent disease and unrest, domestic supplies should
be adecuete for a few months at least, with such supplement
as conditions may indicate.
B. If there is a government or a civil service capable
of functioning, it must be supported by MG in its effort to
secure and distribute supplies end the following steps seem
appropriate:
1) Public announcement - reneated and clear - that the
Jaoanese must live on their own resources, and cannot count
upon imports;
2) Whatever support cn reasonably be given to the
government's efforts to continue rationing and price control
of essential

=== Page 279 === (confidence 0.99)

-11-
of essential goods; to protect against looting; to repair

people from deficit areas to surplus areas; to finance
individual relief; to induce the people to work, by appro-

provide for themselves.
3. Because the currency is likely to have become al-
most worthless, some trade or incentive goods may have to
be o fered to farmers and others for their produce. Such

the principles and nurposes of the occupation, or abandon
the occunation.

CONCLUSIONS
1. Importation of supplies should be avoided, unless
accomplishment of purposes clearly requires imported sup-
plies. The MG function with respect to relief should, if
possible, be restricted to a suring equitable distribution
of supplies produced in Japan.
2. The primary responsibility of providing supplies
to the popul tion shall rest unon the Jananese themselves.
As corroll ry to tis principle, a) this fact should be
iven the widest and most insistent publicity throughout the
country. b) A urance should be given that MG encourages
maximum comestic production, which will be allowed for
civilian use up to the established standard, and
c) MG vill insist upon equitable distribution, and to that
end, will uphold rationing and price control of essential
commodities, and other aupropriate measures.
3. The everage diet of the Japanese shell not exceed
thet of any allied or liberated friendly country.
Tentatively, the caloric content to prevent starvation

ITP - Mr. Wilcox
I have read your memorandum of February 12 on
policy with respect to the occupation of Japan and
your momorandum of July 7 on relief. The former is
Identical with the view that I have been expressing
on the subject; the latter is a good starter on a
problen that will be oxtremely difficult.

In reply refer to
PL
My dear Mr. Anderson:
I have received, by reference from the White House,
your recent letter in which you transmit a suggestiom
for the rehabilitation of Japan.
The spirit which prompted you to submit your
proposal is deeply appreciated. Thank you for your
courtesy in making it available.
Sincerely yours,
For the Secretary of State:
20
J. M. Colton Hand
Chief, Public Views and
Inquiries Section
Division of Public Liaison
Mr. E. A. Anderson,

Hotel Great Northern,
